On 18 February 2026, the leader of the [Burma National Revolutionary Army](71438986.md), [Bo Nagar](70666679.md), surrendered and collaborated with the military. According to multiple reports, Bo Nagar was extracted from his operational area by a military helicopter.[^cite_note-702][^cite_note-703]&#x20;

## Humanitarian impact and war crimes

Main article: [War crimes during the Myanmar civil war (2021–present)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_crimes_during_the_Myanmar_civil_war_\(2021%E2%80%93present\))

See also: [Airstrikes in the Myanmar civil war (2021–present)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airstrikes_in_the_Myanmar_civil_war_\(2021%E2%80%93present\))



A hospital in [Shan State](493376.md) was bombed by [Myanmar Air Force](9484818.md), May 2024

The human rights situation in Myanmar has deteriorated substantially since the beginning of the civil conflict. The Burmese military has escalated its use of [war crimes](48626.md), including murder, mass killings, sexual violence, torture, arbitrary detention, attacks on religious buildings, and targeting civilians.[^cite_note-\:0-704][^cite_note-705][^cite_note-706][^cite_note-707] The junta has also seized the properties of political opponents as part of an intimidation strategy, impacting hundreds of families.[^cite_note-708] [BBC](19344654.md) News reports that the pro-junta paramilitary [Pyusawhti militias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pyusawhti_militias) have been accused of more than one atrocity against civilians.[^cite_note-BBC-losing-24-1-2024-76]&#x20;

Since the onset of the civil conflict, both the Burmese military and resistance forces alike have used educational facilities as bases and detention sites.[^cite_note-\:1b-709] In 2021, over 190 violent attacks on schools were reported in 13 of Myanmar's states and regions.[^cite_note-\:1b-709] As of June 2022, 7.8 million children remained out of school.[^cite_note-710]&#x20;

Myanmar's public health system has effectively collapsed,[^cite_note-711] and the civil war has worsened the country's food security crisis, with one in four people experiencing food insecurity.[^cite_note-712] Poverty and food insecurity have disproportionately affected Myanmar's [Dry Zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dry_Zone_\(Myanmar\)) and the Irrawaddy delta regions, which account for over 80% of the country's agricultural area and are home to a third of the country's population.[^cite_note-713]&#x20;

As of September 2022, 1.3 million people had been internally displaced, and over 13,000 children had been killed.[^cite_note-\:0-704][^cite_note-Mike-714] By March 2023, the UN estimated that since the coup, 17.6 million people in Myanmar required humanitarian assistance, while 1.6 million were internally displaced, and 55,000 civilian buildings had been destroyed.[^cite_note-NN-66]&#x20;

In March 2023 [Volker Türk](71721726.md), the [United Nations High Commissioner for Human Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_High_Commissioner_for_Human_Rights), reported that armed conflict had continued to grow. He stated that they were investigating hundreds of incidents of houses being burnt and civilians, including children, being killed. Overall, 15.2 million people faced [food insecurity](216361.md).[^cite_note-715]&#x20;

In March 2024 [Tom Andrews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Andrews_\(American_politician\)), the [United Nations Special Rapporteur on Human Rights in Myanmar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Special_Rapporteur_on_Human_Rights_in_Myanmar), stated that 18.6 million people were in need of humanitarian aid.[^cite_note-TA-716]&#x20;

On 27 November 2024, the [International Criminal Court](14880.md) (ICC) prosecutor [Karim Ahmad Khan](11606957.md) filed an application for an international arrest warrant for Senior General and Acting President [Min Aung Hlaing](38278640.md).[^cite_note-\:5-717] As the acting president and commander-in-chief of the Myanmar Defence Services, Min Aung Hlaing "bears criminal responsibility for the [crimes against humanity](61099.md) of deportation and persecution of the [Rohingya](1918632.md), committed in Myanmar, and in part in Bangladesh," according to Khan. The arrest warrant request is the first by the ICC in its [Bangladesh/Myanmar investigation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Criminal_Court_investigation_in_Bangladesh/Myanmar).[^cite_note-\:5-717] [Human Rights Watch](46530.md) legal advisor Maria Elena Vignoli described the request as "a strong warning to Myanmar's abusive military leaders that they're not beyond the reach of the law".[^cite_note-718] According to *the Nyan Lin Thit Research Group*, from February 2021 to April 2024, the military junta conducted 2,471 airstrikes across the country which resulted in the deaths of 1,295 civilians including women and children whereas injuring 1,634 others. The attacks destroyed 187 religious buildings, 114 schools, and 39 hospitals.[^cite_note-719]&#x20;

## Economic impact

Economic conditions in Myanmar have substantially worsened due to the ongoing war and to economic mismanagement by the SAC.[^cite_note-720][^cite_note-\:2-721] In 2021, Myanmar's GDP declined by 5.9%.[^cite_note-722] In an interview, Christian Lechervy, the French ambassador to Myanmar, highlighted the impact of the coup on the country's economy: "In 2021, Myanmar's economic growth has contracted by more than 18%, poverty has doubled, the number of people in need of humanitarian aid has multiplied by seven and more than 450,000 people have been forced to flee their homes".[^cite_note-723] Between March and June 2022, almost 10,000 people per month left the country through official channels, worsening the country's [brain drain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brain_drain) and mirroring the civilian exodus that followed the [1962](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1962_Burmese_coup_d%27%C3%A9tat) and [1988 military coups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/8888_Uprising).[^cite_note-Mike-714][^cite_note-\:4-724] The local job market has collapsed.[^cite_note-\:4-724]&#x20;

In September 2022 the G7-led [Financial Action Task Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Financial_Action_Task_Force) announced plans to blacklist Myanmar for failing to stem money laundering and terrorist financing.[^cite_note-\:3-725] At that time, only Iran and North Korea were on the [Financial Action Task Force blacklist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Financial_Action_Task_Force_blacklist).[^cite_note-\:3-725] In October 2022, Myanmar was blacklisted by the task force, which increased volatility in the value of the Burmese kyat.[^cite_note-726]&#x20;

The war disrupted transport and stunted the export of agricultural goods like rice and corn, and the [illegal cultivation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Papaver_somniferum#Restrictions) of [poppy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poppy) became an economic pillar for many Burmese. Myanmar became the world's biggest [opium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opium) producer, producing about 1,080 metric tons in 2023.[^cite_note-727] In parts of the [Dry Zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dry_Zone_\(Myanmar\)) such as [Sagaing Region](493361.md)'s [Ayadaw Township](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayadaw_Township), cannabis supplanted betel nut, cotton, and rice due to economic downturn and rising pest infestation.[^cite_note-728] During the war there has been a "mass refusal" among Myanmar's people to pay taxes and other charges to the junta, leading to a 33% drop in state revenue according to an analysis by the [Special Advisory Council for Myanmar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Advisory_Council_for_Myanmar) (SAC-M). According to the SAC-M, "69% of businesses reported not paying tax to the junta in the first three months of 2022". The cessation of payments of electricity bills by large portions of the population has also significantly cut off the junta's revenue sources.[^cite_note-729]&#x20;

The conflict also facilitated the conditions for the proliferation of [human trafficking](31515227.md) into [fraud factories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fraud_factories) in Myanmar. In these facilities, foreign nationals are trafficked into the country and forced to commit [online scams](805626.md).[^cite_note-730]&#x20;

In August 2024 [CNN](62028.md) published its year-long investigation concluding that approximately half of Myanmar's 54 million inhabitants live below the poverty line. This dire economic situation compels many to resort to extreme measures, including participating in the illicit trade of human organs online. Such transactions can yield an amount equivalent to two years' worth of salary. People often publicize their intent to sell organs on social media platforms, creating a relentless cycle in which families repeatedly turn to these transactions as their financial resources deplete.[^cite_note-731]&#x20;

### Hyperinflation

See also: [Hyperinflation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyperinflation)

By September 2022 the value of the [Burmese kyat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Myanmar_kyat) had depreciated by over 60%,[^cite_note-732] while basic commodity prices had increased by up to 57%.[^cite_note-\:2-721] The [World Bank](36752.md) estimated Myanmar's economy would contract by 18% in 2022.[^cite_note-733] Since April 2022, the country has experienced foreign currency shortages, which have acutely impacted importers, resulting in shortages of basic products like medicines and fertilisers.[^cite_note-734] The military regime has imposed foreign currency controls, which has worsened the shortage of US dollars among international firms operating in the country.[^cite_note-735] Many foreign and multinational companies, including [Telenor](70510.md), [Ooredoo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ooredoo), [Chevron](284749.md), [British American Tobacco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_American_Tobacco) and [Woodside Energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woodside_Energy) have exited the Burmese market as the conflict has intensified.[^cite_note-736]&#x20;

At the end of July 2023 the SAC announced that it would issue a limited number of new 20,000-kyat banknotes. The announcement led to an increase in the price of gold, as well as in foreign currency exchange rates.[^cite_note-737] In March 2024, it was reported that the civil war had [significantly increased prices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyperinflation) of everyday goods, such as rice (160–220%), fuel (520%), and palm oil (75%) from prewar levels. Also, the US dollar to Kyat exchange rate had increased by 160%.[^cite_note-738]&#x20;

In April 2024 the price of gold was around 4.5 million kyat per [*kyattha*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tical_\(unit\)) (a [Burmese unit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Myanmar_units_of_measurement) of mass) compared to 1 million per *kyattha* in February 2021. By May 2024 it was 5.8 million kyat per tical, and by September 2024 it was 7.2 million kyat per tical.[^cite_note-739] The SAC regularly accuses goldsmiths of price manipulation when gold prices rise. An arrest of five traders and the closure of seven shops, caused the price to drop in early April 2024 as traders were fearful of doing business.[^cite_note-740] By May 2024, the U.S. dollar to kyat exchange rate had increased from 1300 before the coup to 5000 on the black market, with the junta reportedly abandoning the fixed exchange rate of 2100.[^cite_note-741][^cite_note-742]&#x20;

### Compensation for casualties

Since the 2021 coup, Myanmar’s military junta has frequently denied or delayed pensions and compensation to families of its own soldiers killed in action. Benefits are often withheld unless the soldier’s body is recovered, leaving many widows without financial support and contributing to school [dropout](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dropping_out) rates for their children. Lower-ranking and conscripted soldiers’ families face the greatest difficulties. Higher-ranked officers’ families, meanwhile, sometimes receive lower compensation than they are entitled to, because the dead officers' military ranks are recorded as lower than those than they actually held.[^cite_note-\:7-743]&#x20;

### Interim Central Bank (ICB)

The [National Unity Government of Myanmar](67413816.md) established an Interim Central Bank (ICB) led by its Planning, Finance and Investment minister, Tin Tun Naing. The goal of establishing this bank is to contest foreign reserves and assets held by the Central Bank in [Naypitaw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naypitaw).[^cite_note-744] It was also reported that the ICB seized 44 billion Kyats from other banks.[^cite_note-745] [Radio Free Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_Free_Asia) explained in regards to Central Banks raising funds for their government; "The NUG has acknowledged raising over \$150 million since the coup" and that while "it dwarfs in comparison to the revenue of the junta, which gave itself a raise of 51 percent in FY2023 to \$2.7 billion—it's not insignificant either."[^cite_note-rfa.org-746]&#x20;

Under the direction of the ICB there is a newly established for-profit bank called Spring Development Bank, with an intent to establish its own cryptocurrency.[^cite_note-rfa.org-746]&#x20;

## Environmental impact

See also: [Deforestation in Myanmar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deforestation_in_Myanmar) and [Climate change in Myanmar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_in_Myanmar)

The deterioration in law and order in many parts of Myanmar has caused "a surge in illegal mining activities" in rural parts of the country. Environmental activists in Myanmar have accused the junta of supporting illegal mining of [rare-earth elements](145440.md) which have "devastating and unpredictable consequences for the region’s ecosystem and inhabitants". Rivers have been contaminated, causing the destruction of local ecosystems, decreasing community access to clean water and disrupting agricultural activities of local farmers. The junta has cracked down on environmental activists who have criticized the government.[^cite_note-747] The conflict has also seen a significant rise in [deforestation in Myanmar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deforestation_in_Myanmar).[^cite_note-748]&#x20;

## Manpower and procurement

### Tatmadaw and allies

See also: [Conscription in Myanmar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conscription_in_Myanmar) and [People's Military Service Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People%27s_Military_Service_Law)

A February 2022 report by [United Nations Special Rapporteur on Human Rights in Myanmar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Special_Rapporteur_on_Human_Rights_in_Myanmar) [Tom Andrews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Andrews_\(American_politician\)) stated that China, Russia, India, [Belarus](3457.md), [Ukraine](31750.md), Israel, [Serbia](29265.md), [Pakistan](23235.md) and [South Korea](27019.md) were selling weapons to the Tatmadaw.[^cite_note-749] The [Karen National Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karen_National_Union) documented the use of North Korean weaponry by the SAC in November 2023.[^cite_note-hwq-750]&#x20;

Anti-SAC forces have claimed that the Tatmadaw has severely struggled with recruitment and morale.[^cite_note-morale-72] The Tatmadaw has adopted [drones](58900.md) in response to rebel groups using drones.[^cite_note-751]&#x20;

On 31 January 2023 the [Ministry of Home Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Home_Affairs_\(Myanmar\)) issued a directive enabling organisations and citizens deemed "loyal to the state," including civilians, civil servants, and army personnel, to obtain [firearms licenses](9971323.md).[^cite_note-752] The regulatory shift has enabled the SAC to arm pro-military [Pyusawhti militias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pyusawhti_militias) and to suppress pro-democracy forces in light of waning military recruitment and their challenges with concurrently operating in multiple [war theatres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theater_\(warfare\)) throughout the country.[^cite_note-753][^cite_note-754] On 12 February 2023, a leaked document purportedly from the [Ministry of Home Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Home_Affairs_\(Myanmar\)) detailed the SAC issuing firearms licenses to pro-military civilians for the operation of counter-insurgency paramilitaries based on the new firearm licensing directive.[^cite_note-755]&#x20;

SAC-aligned Pyusawhti militias have reportedly used force to recruit local men, but have been less than effective in building up grassroots enforcement on behalf of the junta, and have "taken root only in the small number of communities where the military's own party is traditionally strong."[^cite_note-BBC-losing-24-1-2024-76]&#x20;

> One man contacted by the BBC in the area where Wathawa has been mobilising since early 2022 said he had only been able to recruit a maximum of 10–15 men in each village, and then only by threatening to burn down their homes.&#x20;
>
> He said many of the recruits had run away, and were being helped by other villagers to hide from Wathawa and his gun-toting monks.[^cite_note-BBC-losing-24-1-2024-76]

In early December 2023, the Tatmadaw-led government appealed for deserters to return, promising them exoneration. The [National Unity Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Unity_Government) claimed that 20,000 soldiers who had deserted had rejoined its ranks.[^cite_note-756] By 7 December, the junta began freeing soldiers who had been jailed for desertion to ease apparent manpower shortages as a result of battlefield pressures from recent operations.[^cite_note-757]&#x20;

On 10 February 2024, the Tatmadaw announced the [People's Military Service Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People%27s_Military_Service_Law), requiring all men aged 18 to 35 and women aged 18 to 27 to complete up to two years of [mandatory military service](5735.md), amid its territorial losses. Those who fail to enlist face imprisonment for up to five years during a national emergency.[^cite_note-758] This announcement has been interpreted by some as a sign of increasing desperation in the face of steadily advancing resistance forces.[^cite_note-759] In the wake of the announcement, [Deutsche Welle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deutsche_Welle) also reported that "thousands" of young people were fleeing to [Thailand](30128.md) to [evade conscription](472220.md) before it came into effect.[^cite_note-760] Local Myanmar government officials are reportedly [extorting](162449.md) bribes from those seeking to [avoid being conscripted](472220.md).[^cite_note-761] 21 administrators in Rakhine's [Thandwe](2969521.md) announced their resignation in response to the military recruitment.[^cite_note-762] Rebel groups killed at least 37 local officials carrying out the junta's conscription efforts.[^cite_note-763]&#x20;

Activist [Thinzar Shunlei Yi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thinzar_Shunlei_Yi) thought the military's historic "indoctrination techniques are deeply rooted in nationalism and religious ideologies" leaving "soldiers and their families feeling disoriented amidst shifting societal paradigms".[^cite_note-764]&#x20;

### Anti-junta forces

The limited possession of guns by ethnic insurgent movements along with the lack of international support and formal means of acquiring military material has presented the anti-junta forces with a challenging situation for the confrontation of the military regime. Faced with this difficulty since the early stages of the armed insurgency, the resistance movement sought ways to manufacture the necessary weapons and equipment for the conflict. Initially, the rebels expanded the production of a traditionally made, [single-shot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Single-shot) rifle known as *Tumi*, especially in the [Chin state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chin_state). Nonetheless, this kind of rifle is severely limited for battleground action. For this reason, the fighters have developed alternative models which are more advanced, while still calling them *Tumi*.[^cite_note-765] Since then, the resistance movement has developed many kinds of [carbines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbines), [landmines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landmines) and bomb drones, to be manufactured within the technological and material means of liberated territories and underground cells.[^cite_note-defensep1-766][^cite_note-767][^cite_note-768]&#x20;

Commercially available drones rigged to carry bombs were used to attack military positions. PDF groups reportedly produced naval bombs to target government logistics in rivers. Meanwhile, defected soldiers developed 60 mm long-range [mortars](324499.md). The use of [3D printing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/3D_printing) was also reported, both to salvage weapons taken from the junta and for the improvised production of [FGC-9](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FGC-9) semiautomatic carbines.[^cite_note-defensep1-766][^cite_note-769] An arms trafficker in possession of [nuclear materials](2181563.md) was found working with an unnamed insurgent ethnic armed group in Myanmar.[^cite_note-hwq-750][^cite_note-770] The success of [Operation 1027](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_1027) enabled the Brotherhood Alliance to seize enormous caches of arms and ammunition from the Tatmadaw, making it better equipped than before it launched Operation 1027.[^cite_note-771]&#x20;

On 20 August 2025, the [United Wa State Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Wa_State_Army) declared that they would no longer provide weapons or financial aid to the [Ta'ang National Liberation Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ta%27ang_National_Liberation_Army), [Myanmar National Democratic Alliance Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Myanmar_National_Democratic_Alliance_Army), and [Shan State Army (SSPP)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shan_State_Army_\(SSPP\)).[^cite_note-772] Speculation arose that the UWSA's announcement, combined with Tatmadaw offenses in Shan State, contributed to a rise in prices for weapons and ammunition in the supply chains of the anti-junta insurgencies.[^cite_note-773] According to [the Irrawaddy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Irrawaddy), the [Arakan Army](38106209.md) has been regarded as the strongest anti-junta ethnic armed organisation in the country; while the UWSA is believed to have greater financing and equipment, the AA is estimated to have over 50,000 personnel, making it the largest active ethnic army in the country.[^cite_note-774]&#x20;

## Foreign involvement and spillover

Main article: [Foreign involvement in the Myanmar civil war (2021–present)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_involvement_in_the_Myanmar_civil_war_\(2021%E2%80%93present\))

See also: [Spillover of the Myanmar civil war (2021–present)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spillover_of_the_Myanmar_civil_war_\(2021%E2%80%93present\)) and [Involvement of Northeast Indian insurgents in the Myanmar civil war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Involvement_of_Northeast_Indian_insurgents_in_the_Myanmar_civil_war)



NUG's UN Ambassador [Kyaw Moe Tun](66913798.md) talks in an interview in 2022.

In June 2021 the [United Nations General Assembly](31957.md) passed a non-binding resolution asking member states to impose an arms embargo on Myanmar.[^cite_note-775] Two hundred international organisations, including [Amnesty International](18947898.md) and [Human Rights Watch](46530.md) have continued to press the UN and its member states to adopt a global arms embargo.[^cite_note-776][^cite_note-777] The United States, the United Kingdom, Canada, and the European Union have, in response to the ongoing violence, sanctioned individuals and organisations associated with the Burmese military.[^cite_note-778][^cite_note-\:6-779] However, the effectiveness of these sanctions has been undermined by poor coordination among governments and the lack of sanctions against high-impact targets.[^cite_note-\:6-779]&#x20;



Myanmar absent at the US-ASEAN Summit 2022 in Washington, D.C.

[ASEAN](106539.md) blocked Myanmar from participating in regional summits after the 2021 coup.[^cite_note-\:10-780] However, this was reversed after New Zealand invited junta officials to two [ASEAN Summits](3350351.md) hosted in [Wellington](33804.md) in April 2024.[^cite_note-781][^cite_note-782] ASEAN member states have not taken a consistent, coordinated approach with respect to the ongoing civil war, due to internal divisions. Brunei, Indonesia, Malaysia, the Philippines and Singapore are strongly opposed to the military junta,[^cite_note-783][^cite_note-\:11-784] but [Thailand](30128.md) was a key ally of the junta until the election of [Srettha Thavisin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Srettha_Thavisin) as prime minister.[^cite_note-785][^cite_note-786] After the election of [Anutin Charnvirakul](60867789.md) as prime minister, the Thai government has reapproached Myanmar, bolstering the international legitimacy of the Burmese government. Newly inaugurated president [Min Aung Hlaing](38278640.md) arrived in Bangkok, on a landmark visit, on the 6th of April, 2026.[^cite_note-787]&#x20;

India, which represents Myanmar's fourth-largest export market and fifth-largest import partner, has continued a business-as-usual approach to cross-border relations and continues to recognize the military junta.[^cite_note-enablerule-788] State-owned and private Indian companies supply arms and raw materials to the junta.[^cite_note-789] On the other hand, [India had hostile relations with China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China%E2%80%93India_relations) for the first several years of the war, which in part may have contributed to India's ongoing support for the junta as a balance attempt to prevent Chinese encroachment, which is met with criticism.[^cite_note-790][^cite_note-791][^cite_note-792] Through the regional governments of [Manipur](422642.md) and [Mizoram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mizoram), India also established de-facto relations with Chin and Rakhine rebels, while also reinforcing the border with [Assam Rifles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assam_Rifles) paramilitary soldiers.[^cite_note-793]&#x20;

[Bangladesh](3454.md) recognizes the military junta, but does not support its actions, in part because the [Rohingya genocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rohingya_genocide) has led to more than 1 million [Rohingya refugees fleeing to Bangladesh](53544313.md).[^cite_note-794]Its position in the conflict has been informed by repeated [spillovers of the conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spillover_of_the_Myanmar_civil_war_\(2021%E2%80%93present\)) into its territory.[^cite_note-new-795] After the entire Bangladesh-Myanmar came under the control of the [Arakan Army](38106209.md), the Bengali government initiated informal relations with the militant organisation, while also trying to clamp down on Rohingya groups operating on their territory.[^cite_note-796]&#x20;

As of December 2023 [East Timor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Timor) remains the only government to have openly expressed sympathies to the anti-regime forces in Myanmar.[^cite_note-797] In August 2023, the [State Administration Council](66589972.md) expelled the East Timorese ambassador in retaliation for the East Timorese government meeting with the NUG.[^cite_note-798]&#x20;



In 2023, Director of the [Office of the Central Foreign Affairs Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Office_of_the_Central_Foreign_Affairs_Commission) [Wang Yi](18211536.md) and Myanmar's Foreign Minister [Than Swe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Than_Swe_\(diplomat\)) jointly meet the press after the eighth [LMC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lancang-Mekong_Cooperation) Foreign Ministers' Meeting.

Since the coup d'état, China and Russia have supported the military junta and have been its main arms suppliers. China is Myanmar's largest trading partner.[^cite_note-France2420231031-799][^cite_note-800] The two countries have blocked any substantive action against Myanmar's military at the United Nations Security Council, while Myanmar's security forces have reportedly used Chinese and Russian-supplied weapons to perpetrate human rights violations.[^cite_note-enablerule-788] Chinese support for the junta and rebel groups in [Shan State](493376.md) has led to a rise in popular [anti-Chinese sentiment in Myanmar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Chinese_sentiment_in_Myanmar).[^cite_note-morris-801][^cite_note-802] However, the fact that the Three Brotherhood Alliance's [Operation 1027](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_1027) in late 2023 was carried out near the [China–Myanmar border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China%E2%80%93Myanmar_border) indicated an initial shift in China's stance,[^cite_note-803] which was attributed by analysts to concerns about cyber-scam centers, the pursuit of favorable concessions from the junta on the [China-Myanmar Economic Corridor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China-Myanmar_Economic_Corridor), and the opportunity to influence the PDF in light of evolving dynamics between NUG and EAO groups.[^cite_note-804] Since 2024, Beijing has played a key role in reintegrating Myanmar back into the international community, pressuring rebel groups operating in Shan State to reopen the Muse-Mandalay road, a key logistical artery for Chinese imports/exports and the wider [Belt and Road Initiative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belt_and_Road_Initiative).[^cite_note-805][^cite_note-806] In a video recorded by pro-Tatmadaw media during the [2025 Tianjin SCO summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Tianjin_SCO_summit), Min Aung Hlaing personally thanked China for "exerting pressure on the armed groups in the border regions for the development of northern Myanmar."[^cite_note-807]&#x20;



Min Aung Hlaing meets Head Rais [Rustam Minnikhanov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rustam_Minnikhanov) of [Tatarstan](67135.md) in Russia, June 2021.

Russia has embraced deeper ties with the Burmese military junta as the civil war has progressed. Russia has provided [materiel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Materiel), military training for over 50 [Myanmar Air Force](9484818.md) pilots, and diplomatic backing to the regime.[^cite_note-808] Min Aung Hlaing has visited Russia several times, personally meeting with Russian president [Vladimir Putin](32817.md) in September 2022. The military junta backed the [Russian invasion of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine).[^cite_note-\:10-780] Russia was among the few countries[^cite_note-809] to send a congratulatory message to the junta on Myanmar's [Independence Day](34274976.md).[^cite_note-810] In March 2024, Special Rapporteur Tom Andrews saw Russia as still the junta's top source of weapons.[^cite_note-TA-716]&#x20;

Although small in number, foreign volunteers serving with anti-junta forces have been documented. One such volunteer, going under the [nom de guerre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nom_de_guerre) Azad, joined the [Anti-Fascist Internationalist Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Fascist_Internationalist_Front) to assist fighters in the [Chin theater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chin_theater). Azad gained military experience while fighting in the Syrian [Rojava conflict](48303468.md).[^cite_note-811][^cite_note-812][^cite_note-813] Foreign volunteers are also present in [Karen State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karen_State), with most being members of the humanitarian [Free Burma Rangers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_Burma_Rangers) organisation.[^cite_note-814] While FBR associates use weapons only for [self-defence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Self-defence), some members have joined local rebel groups fighting the Tatmadaw, offering their expertise in many fields, such as drone technology, as is the case with the Federal Wings, a group that contributes to rebel drone [R\&D](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R%26D).[^cite_note-815] The [KNLA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KNLA) is also one of the few EAO's to accept [foreign volunteers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dave_Everett) in their rankings, a tradition that dates back to the [Cold War](325329.md) era in Burma. International volunteers in Karen State have justified their presence for a number of reasons, including: defending Kayin [Christians](18337522.md), assisting rebel groups and contributing to the reinstallation of a [democratic regime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_regime) in Myanmar.&#x20;
